```text
458	Top-5 feature importance is identical across seeds (\texttt{var\_surprisal}, \texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_ppl}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}); depth signal therefore arises from the recursive process itself rather than idiosyncratic LoRA weight configurations.
459	
460	\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}
461	\label{app:length_ablation}
462	
463	Table~\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).
464	
465	\begin{table}[h]
466	\centering
467	\small
468	\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}
469	\label{tab:length_ablation}
470	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
471	\toprule
472	Passage length (tokens) & Acc (\%) \\
473	\midrule
474	128 & 68.35 \\
475	256 & 73.80 \\
476	512 & 77.82 \\
477	\bottomrule
478	\end{tabular}
479	\end{table}
480	
481	Depth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.
482	The monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.
483	
484	\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}
485	\label{app:mixed_depth}
486	
487	Per-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\,pp tolerance).
488	Individual class deltas range from $-$0.45\,pp to $-$2.43\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift); the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.
489	Macro-recall $\Delta$ = $-$1.62\,pp (within the 2\,pp threshold); individual class variations (max 2.43\,pp on d1) arise from held-out sample composition rather than classifier sensitivity to corpus distribution, as the Random Forest produces identical per-sample predictions regardless of batch context.
490	
491	\subsection{Learned Estimator Comparison}
492	\label{app:learned_estimator}
493	
494	Table~\ref{tab:learned_estimator} evaluates three learned classifiers (XGBoost, ordinal MLP with CORAL loss, prototypical networks) under the same leave-one-model-out protocol as Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}.
495	All three methods operate on the same 19-dimensional feature vector; the comparison isolates the effect of the classification head.
496	
497	\begin{table}[h]
498	\centering
499	\small
500	\caption{Learned estimator cross-model transfer (leave-one-model-out, nucleus, 4-class). RF baseline from Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}. Chance = 25\%.}
501	\label{tab:learned_estimator}
502	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
503	\toprule
504	Method & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL & Mean \\
505	\midrule
506	RF (baseline) & --- & --- & --- & 66.4 \\
507	XGBoost & 63.7 & 69.8 & 68.1 & 67.2 \\
508	Ordinal MLP & 62.5 & 69.7 & 67.9 & 66.7 \\
509	Prototypical & 61.6 & 70.0 & 68.4 & 66.6 \\
510	\bottomrule
511	\end{tabular}
512	\end{table}
513	
514	All three learned methods match the RF baseline within 0.8\,pp on average; the Pythia holdout is consistently the hardest (61.6--63.7\%), while OLMo and GPT-2~XL holdouts yield 67.9--70.0\%.
515	The narrow spread ($\leq$0.8\,pp across four methods) reinforces the conclusion that the 19-feature representation, not the classifier, determines the accuracy ceiling in the cross-model transfer setting.
516	
517	\subsection{Instruction-Tuned Model at 1.5B Scale}
518	\label{app:instruct_1p5b}
519	
520	To test whether instruction tuning eliminates depth signal at matched scale (1.5B, avoiding the quantization confound of \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), we run the full recursive pipeline on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with self-reference feature extraction (i.e., the same model serves as both generator and reference).
521	
522	The RF classifier achieves 47.4\% four-class accuracy (95\% CI $[46.7, 48.1]$, $1.9\times$ chance); instruction tuning therefore reduces but does not eliminate depth signal.
523	The pairwise AUC profile differs from base models: d0v1 = 0.653, d1v2 = 0.620, d2v3 = 0.664, with a gap-dependent pattern (non-adjacent pairs yield higher AUC, e.g., d0v3 = 0.902) but no pair reaching the 0.75 threshold.
524	
525	The top-5 features shift substantially: \texttt{surprisal\_range}, \texttt{surprisal\_position\_corr}, \texttt{prob\_std}, \texttt{surprisal\_first\_half\_minus\_second}, and \texttt{surprisal\_std} replace the perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{var\_surprisal}) that dominate in base models.
526	This feature shift suggests instruction tuning compresses the perplexity distribution (reducing tail discriminability) while preserving positional and range-based surprisal patterns.
527	
528	Two caveats apply: (1) self-reference evaluation may inflate or deflate accuracy relative to cross-reference evaluation (cf.\ \S\ref{app:ref_ablation}); (2) a single instruction-tuned model does not establish the generality of the feature shift across architectures or instruction-tuning recipes.
529	
530	\subsection{Downstream Fine-Tuning Probe}
531	\label{app:downstream}
532	
533	We fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$) on depth-stratified subsets (500 samples each) and evaluate held-out perplexity (200 samples, 50 per depth).
534	
535	\begin{table}[h]
536	\centering
537	\small
538	\caption{Downstream fine-tuning probe: held-out perplexity by training subset (Pythia-410M LoRA, seed=42). $\Delta$: signed difference vs.\ baseline (negative = better).}
539	\label{tab:downstream}
540	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
541	\toprule
542	Training subset & PPL & $\Delta$ vs.\ baseline \\
543	\midrule
544	Baseline (no fine-tuning) & 5.67 & --- \\
545	d0 only & 5.65 & $-$0.02 \\
546	d1 only & 5.55 & $-$0.12 \\
547	d2 only & 5.56 & $-$0.11 \\
548	Mixed (d0--d3) & 5.55 & $-$0.12 \\
549	\bottomrule
550	\end{tabular}
551	\end{table}
552	
553	The hypothesis that d0-only data yields the lowest perplexity is not supported: d1-only and mixed subsets tie for best (PPL~5.55), both marginally below d0-only (5.65).
554	The PPL spread is small (0.12, 2.1\% relative) and the sample size (500 per condition, single seed) is insufficient for statistical significance.
555	Training loss decreases monotonically with depth (d0: 2.558, d1: 1.920, d2: 1.286), consistent with recursive generation reducing information content and making text easier to model.
556	This pilot establishes that depth-filtered data is not harmful for downstream fine-tuning; whether depth-aware curation provides a meaningful advantage requires larger-scale evaluation.
557	
558	\subsection{Feature Group Ablation}
559	\label{app:feature_ablation}
560	
561	Table~\ref{tab:feature_ablation} reports cross-model transfer accuracy (leave-one-model-out, nucleus) when restricting the classifier to subsets of the 19-feature vector, grouped by signal type.
562	
563	\begin{table}[h]
564	\centering
565	\small
566	\caption{Feature group ablation (cross-model LOO, nucleus, 4-class). Chance = 25\%.}
567	\label{tab:feature_ablation}
568	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
569	\toprule
570	Feature group & \#Feat. & Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ vs.\ Full \\
571	\midrule
572	Full (all 19) & 19 & 66.6 & --- \\
573	Perplexity only & 9 & 66.0 & $-$0.5 \\
574	Entropy/Surprisal only & 5 & 63.2 & $-$3.4 \\
575	Repetition/Diversity only & 5 & 53.0 & $-$13.6 \\
576	\midrule
577	\texttt{p90\_ppl} only & 1 & 58.3 & $-$8.3 \\
578	\bottomrule
579	\end{tabular}
580	\end{table}
581	
582	The 9 perplexity features nearly match the full set ($\Delta{=}{-}0.5$\,pp), while repetition/diversity features alone fall 13.6\,pp below; perplexity degradation statistics therefore carry the dominant depth signal.
583	The top-2 individual features by Gini importance are \texttt{p90\_ppl} (22.4\%) and \texttt{var\_surprisal} (18.8\%), accounting for 41\% of total importance.
584	
585	\subsection{RF Seed Robustness}
586	\label{app:rf_seed}
587	
588	Table~\ref{tab:rf_seed} reports cross-model transfer accuracy across three RF random seeds.
589	
590	\begin{table}[h]
591	\centering
592	\small
593	\caption{RF seed robustness (cross-model LOO, nucleus, 4-class).}
594	\label{tab:rf_seed}
595	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
596	\toprule
597	Seed & Acc (\%) \\
598	\midrule
599	42 & 66.4 \\
600	123 & 66.5 \\
601	456 & 66.5 \\
602	\midrule
603	Mean $\pm$ std & 66.48 $\pm$ 0.06 \\
604	\bottomrule
605	\end{tabular}
606	\end{table}
607	
608	The 0.06\,pp standard deviation across seeds confirms that RF classification is deterministic to within rounding noise at this sample size, and reported accuracy differences across experimental conditions (e.g., feature ablation, cross-model transfer) are not attributable to classifier randomness.
609	
610	\subsection{Binary Detector Baselines for Ordinal Classification}
611	\label{app:detector_baselines}
612	
613	We evaluate whether existing binary AI-text detectors, when their scores are used as features for ordinal classification, can match our 19-feature RF pipeline.
614	Binoculars~\cite{binoculars2024} and Fast-DetectGPT~\cite{fast_detectgpt2024} scores are computed on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) subset and fed to: (a)~a log-likelihood rank classifier (Log-Rank, 64.0\%), (b)~a combined RF trained on both detector scores (Combined, 74.2\%), and (c)~the standard 19-feature RF (78.2\%).
615	The 4\,pp gap (74.2\% vs.\ 78.2\%) confirms that perplexity distributional features capture ordinal depth structure beyond what generic detection scores provide.
616	Note that Fast-DetectGPT exhibits numerical overflow on Pythia nucleus d1 (mean perturbation score $>$28{,}000); results for this cell should be interpreted cautiously.
617	
618	\subsection{Prompted Generation Results}
619	\label{app:prompted_gen}
620	
621	Table~\ref{tab:prompted_gen} reports depth estimation performance under topic-seeded (prompted) generation using GPT-2~XL with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, depths 0--3).
622	Generation is conditioned on Wikipedia topic prompts rather than a bare BOS token, a more ecologically valid test of depth signal persistence.
623	
624	\begin{table}[h]
625	\centering
626	\small
627	\caption{Prompted generation (GPT-2~XL, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, topic-seeded). 5-fold CV accuracy and pairwise AUC. Chance = 25\%.}
628	\label{tab:prompted_gen}
629	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
630	\toprule
631	Metric & Value \\
632	\midrule
633	4-class accuracy (\%) & 69.52 $[68.9, 70.2]$ \\
634	BOS-seeded baseline (\%) & 83.3 \\
635	$\Delta$ (prompted $-$ BOS) & $-$13.78\,pp \\
636	\midrule
637	AUC d0v1 & 0.916 \\
638	AUC d1v2 & 0.782 \\
639	AUC d2v3 & 0.744 \\
640	\midrule
641	Top-1 feature & \texttt{p90\_ppl} \\
642	Top-2 feature & \texttt{var\_surprisal} \\
643	Top-3 feature & \texttt{p75\_ppl} \\
644	\bottomrule
645	\end{tabular}
646	\end{table}
647	
648	The 13.8\,pp drop relative to BOS-seeded generation is expected: topic prompts constrain the output distribution, reducing the variance that distinguishes depths.
649	Nevertheless, accuracy remains $2.8\times$ chance and pairwise AUC decays monotonically (0.916, 0.782, 0.744); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of unconstrained generation.
650	Top features are identical to BOS-seeded (\texttt{p90\_ppl} first); the same perplexity tail erosion mechanism operates under prompted conditions.
651	
652	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
653	\label{app:extended_discussion}
654	
655	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
656	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
657	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
658	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.
659	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
660	
661	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
662	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
663	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
664	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
665	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
666	A layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
667	
```

high-entropy output resists memorization, so depth-dependent distributional shifts do not accumulate.\nThis establishes \\emph{memorization as a prerequisite} for detectable depth signal: recursive depth is discriminable only when successive fine-tuning iterations progressively reshape the output distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$\\tau$ non-transferability.}\nLeave-one-$\\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross}), worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\\%; Train on three temperatures, test on the held-out one.}\n\\label{tab:multi_tau_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc (\\%) & d1v2 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\tau{=}0.5$ & 52.2 & 0.176 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.7$ & 41.0 & 0.299 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}0.9$ & 46.4 & 0.277 \\\\\n$\\tau{=}1.1$ & 47.1 & 0.525 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 46.7 & 0.319 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features via linear probing of [CLS] representations from DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base.\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics; PLM columns report F1; RF d2 column reports recall (the class with the largest PLM--RF gap). Both PLM backbones share the same failure pattern: collapsed d1/d2 scores despite adequate d0/d3.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_detail}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Acc (\\%) & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeBERTa-v3 & 58.3\\,$\\pm$\\,4.7 & .947 & .378 & .247 & .656 \\\\\nRoBERTa-base & 59.5\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & .958 & .416 & .261 & .647 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 feat.) & 78.7 & --- & --- & .596$^\\dagger$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Recall; shown for the depth class with the largest PLM--RF gap.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature space divergence.}\nSHAP TreeExplainer analysis confirms that perplexity and surprisal features account for 88.8\\% of RF's total contribution.\nThree independent importance measures (SHAP, Gini, and permutation) show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\nLinear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}); pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics, a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods; depth estimation therefore benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\nThis comparison is bounded by backbone scale ($\\leq$125M parameters); whether larger PLMs (e.g., 7B+) can close the gap via richer internal representations remains an open question.\n\n\\subsection{Domain Bias Analysis}\n\\label{app:domain_bias}\n\nTo assess whether the classifier exploits depth-related features or memorizes stylistic cues from individual seed articles, we conduct three sub-experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological note.}\nAn initial implementation assigned sample IDs with a global counter across depths, inadvertently correlating seed-group identity with depth (LOGO accuracy 64.0\\%, seed-group prediction 20.8\\%).\nWe discovered the issue during internal review, corrected to per-depth seed assignment ensuring each group contains equal samples per depth, and re-ran all sub-experiments.\nThe results below use the corrected pipeline; the initial (buggy) numbers are reported for transparency.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-A: Standard CV.}\nFive-fold CV accuracy on 3-class (d1/d2/d3) classification ranges from 59\\% to 82\\% across the 9 atlas cells (up to 2.5$\\times$ above the 33.3\\% chance level); depth signal is therefore the dominant classification factor.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-B: LOGO evaluation.}\nWe assign each passage to one of 10 seed-article groups (balanced: each group contains equal samples per depth) and perform leave-one-group-out CV.\nLOGO mean accuracy is 72.1\\% (range 59.5--81.6\\%), matching standard CV (72.0\\%) within noise (before fix: 64.0\\%, reflecting the depth--group confound).\nPer-cell fold standard deviations range from 0.4pp to 1.4pp; cross-group generalization is therefore as reliable as within-group classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Sub-C: Negative control.}\nWe train two classifiers on identical features: one predicting depth, the other predicting seed-group identity (10-class, chance = 10\\%).\nDepth prediction accuracy averages 72.0\\%; seed-group prediction averages 10.2\\%, indistinguishable from chance (before fix: 20.8\\%, inflated by the depth--group confound).\nThe depth-to-seed-group accuracy ratio is 7.1$\\times$; the feature representation therefore encodes depth but not seed-article identity.\n\nTaken together, these results show that seed-article grouping has no measurable effect on depth estimation after correcting the group assignment: LOGO accuracy equals standard CV, and seed-group prediction is at chance.\n\n\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n\\label{app:calibration}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages, crawled 2019--2020; pre-LLM deployment).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wild-data d0 accuracy and FPR for nucleus RF classifiers. All passages are human-written (true d0). FPR = fraction misclassified as d1+. 95\\% Wilson CIs computed for C4 ($n{=}5{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Source & $n$ & d0 (\\%) & FPR (\\%) & 95\\% Wilson CI \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & WikiText & 546 & 100.0 & 0.0 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 79.8 & 20.2 & [19.1, 21.3] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nGPT-2~XL & WikiText & 546 & 99.3 & 0.7 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 77.6 & 22.4 & [21.3, 23.6] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nOLMo-1B & WikiText & 546 & 98.5 & 1.5 & --- \\\\\n & C4 & 5{,}000 & 95.3 & 4.7 & [4.2, 5.3] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn WikiText-103, all three classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy; editorial text is therefore reliably distinguished from recursive generations across model families.\nOn C4, FPR varies sharply: OLMo (4.7\\%) vs.\\ Pythia (20.2\\%) and GPT-2~XL (22.4\\%).\nC4 misclassifications concentrate in template and SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0 passages), rather than in random noise.\nThe model gap likely reflects training data composition: OLMo (trained on Dolma, which includes diverse web data) produces d0 features more tolerant of web-crawl variation, while Pythia and GPT-2~XL (trained primarily on Wikipedia/WebText) map template-heavy content into the d1+ region.\nAmong C4 false positives for GPT-2~XL, predictions distribute as d1 (9.0\\%), d2 (3.7\\%), d3 (9.6\\%); for OLMo, d1 (2.9\\%), d2 (0.5\\%), d3 (1.3\\%). The non-uniform depth distribution suggests the classifier maps specific repetition patterns to particular depth signatures rather than producing random misclassifications.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Curation PoC}\n\\label{app:curation_poc}\n\nTo validate that depth estimation translates to actionable data curation, we conduct a proof-of-concept downstream evaluation.\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) for 3 epochs on 8 data conditions derived from the Pythia nucleus depth-estimation corpus, repeating each condition over 5 random seeds.\nConditions vary the selection mechanism (RF-predicted depth, oracle depth, random subsampling) and stringency (strict $\\approx$25\\%, moderate $\\approx$50\\%, soft threshold).\nTable~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Curation PoC: per-condition perplexity (Pythia-410M LoRA, 5 seeds). $\\Delta$ is signed so that positive values indicate lower (better) perplexity than unfiltered. $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$ (paired $t$-test vs.\\ unfiltered).}\n\\label{tab:curation_poc}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Mean PPL & Std & $n$ & $\\Delta$ vs Unfilt. & $p$ & $d_z$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nunfiltered        & 15.776 & 0.066 & 10{,}000 & ---      & ---   & ---      \\\\\nrf\\_soft          & 15.733 & 0.038 &  4{,}422 & +0.043   & 0.407 &  0.41    \\\\\nrf\\_moderate      & 15.756 & 0.056 &  4{,}940 & +0.021   & 0.492 &  0.34    \\\\\nrf\\_strict        & 15.810 & 0.061 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.034 & 0.607 & $-$0.25  \\\\\noracle\\_moderate  & 15.809 & 0.103 &  5{,}000 & $-$0.033 & 0.485 & $-$0.34  \\\\\noracle\\_strict    & 15.879 & 0.128 &  2{,}500 & $-$0.103 & 0.216 & $-$0.66  \\\\\nrandom\\_soft      & 16.062 & 0.079 &  4{,}422 & $-$0.286 & ${<}$0.001$^*$ & $-$2.77  \\\\\nrandom\\_moderate  & 15.998 & 0.090 &  4{,}940 & $-$0.222 & 0.028$^*$ & $-$1.50  \\\\\nrandom\\_strict    & 16.239 & 0.165 &  2{,}496 & $-$0.463 & 0.013$^*$ & $-$1.89  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise comparisons (paired $t$-test, 5 seeds). $^*$\\,: $p<0.05$; $^{**}$\\,: $p<0.01$.}\n\\label{tab:curation_comparisons}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nComparison & $\\Delta$ PPL & $t$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nrf\\_strict vs random\\_strict     & 0.429 & $-$7.05 & 0.002$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_moderate vs random\\_moderate & 0.242 & $-$3.77 & 0.020$^*$    \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs random\\_soft         & 0.328 & $-$17.98 & ${<}$0.001$^{**}$ \\\\\nrf\\_soft vs unfiltered           & 0.043 &    0.93 & 0.407        \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Reference Model Ablation}\n\\label{app:ref_ablation}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Reference model ablation. Classification accuracy (5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B generated text (nucleus, d0--d3) using different reference models for feature extraction.}\n\\label{tab:ref_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nReference Model & Architecture & Accuracy (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B (self) & GPT-NeoX & 78.6 $\\pm$ 0.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B (cross) & OLMo & 74.5 $\\pm$ 0.7 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL (cross) & GPT-2 & 67.3 $\\pm$ 0.7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Scale Full Results}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_full}\n\nThis section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.\nWe extend the benchmark to two 7--8B models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, both under 4-bit NF4 QLoRA fine-tuning, generating depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\ldots, 5\\}$ with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth).\nFeature extraction uses the same reference model (Pythia-1.4B) and 19-feature pipeline as all prior experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-scale comparison: 4-class RF accuracy (\\%, depths 0--3, nucleus), $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ (depths 0--5), and d0-vs-d1 AUC. CI: 95\\% bootstrap. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & 4-class acc & $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70}$ & d0v1 AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL (1.5B) & 84.1 & 4 & 0.999 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 78.9 & 4 & 0.997 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 78.7 & 4 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 70.2\\,[68.3, 72.4] & 4 & 0.949 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 60.1\\,[58.0, 62.1] & ${>}5$ & 0.969 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}\nBoth 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\\% (95\\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$); recursive depth therefore remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\\times$ larger than the reference model.\nDepth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).\n\n\\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}\nAt threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$, exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.\nBoth larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Consecutive binary AUC for 7--8B models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$). Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic decay (d4v5 drops below $T{=}0.70$); Qwen2.5-7B shows a non-monotonic (U-shaped) pattern with all pairs above $T$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & d0v1 & d1v2 & d2v3 & d3v4 & d4v5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & 0.949 & 0.877 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & 0.969 & 0.729 & 0.709 & 0.732 & 0.772 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(3) Divergent AUC trajectories and instruction-tuning effect.}\nAmong instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).\nA base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\\% four-class accuracy (95\\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\\% with strictly monotonic decay.\nThe base-model control experiment suggests instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds the depth signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise AUC: Llama-3.1-8B Base vs.\\ Instruct.}\n\\label{tab:llama3_base_auc}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nVariant & $d_{0\\text{v}1}$ & $d_{1\\text{v}2}$ & $d_{2\\text{v}3}$ & $d_{3\\text{v}4}$ & $d_{4\\text{v}5}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase & 0.918 & 0.794 & 0.682 & 0.730 & 0.595 \\\\\nInstruct & 0.965 & 0.874 & 0.771 & 0.716 & 0.618 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe base model exhibits non-monotonic decay with a U-shaped recovery at $d_{3\\text{v}4}$, while the instruction-tuned variant shows strictly monotonic decay. Both variants exceed chance (AUC $> 0.5$) at all boundaries; depth signal exists regardless of instruction-tuning, though instruction-tuning strengthens and regularizes it.\n\n\\paragraph{Caveats.}\nFor Qwen2.5-7B, the CI lower bound (58.0\\%) does not exclude sub-60\\% accuracy; Llama-3.1-8B's lower bound (68.3\\%) is more comfortably above chance but still below the 75--83\\% range at 1--1.5B.\nThe divergent AUC trajectories across two models caution against strong generalizations from only two 7--8B data points: both the monotonic (Llama-3.1) and non-monotonic (Qwen2.5) patterns are single observations, and further architectures are needed to establish which is the dominant regime at this scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds.}\n\\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}\nFour confounds limit these results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling, leaving other strategies untested at this scale; (4)~vocabulary mismatch between 7--8B generators and the 1.4B reference model.\nThe base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.\nThe remaining confounds are not mutually independent (quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution); it is therefore infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\\% vs.\\ 78--85\\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Boundary Generalization}\n\\label{app:cross_boundary}\n\nThe preceding experiments assume a single model family generates all recursive depths.\nIn practice, recursive contamination may traverse model boundaries: text generated by model~A is used to train model~B, whose output trains model~C.\nWe probe whether the depth signal persists under two relaxations of the single-model assumption.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model recursive chains.}\nWe construct a heterogeneous chain: human text (d0) $\\to$ Pythia-1.4B generates d1 $\\to$ OLMo-1B finetuned on Pythia-d1 generates d2 $\\to$ GPT-2~XL finetuned on OLMo-d2 generates d3.\nEach recursive step uses a \\emph{different} model family, eliminating the possibility that depth signal arises from a single model's idiosyncratic behavior.\nA 4-class Random Forest achieves 84.75\\% accuracy (95\\% CI $[81.0, 88.25]$), with per-depth F1 scores of 0.98 (d0), 0.88 (d1), 0.76 (d2), and 0.76 (d3).\nPairwise AUC remains high: d0-vs-d1 = 0.999 and d2-vs-d3 = 0.841.\nThese results suggest that distributional artifacts of recursive generation persist across model boundaries. This constitutes preliminary evidence for a process-level depth signal rather than individual generator artifacts.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound discussion.}\nThe heterogeneous chain confounds architecture identity with depth (d1=Pythia, d2=OLMo, d3=GPT-2~XL).\nHowever, if the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, near-perfect accuracy would be expected since architecture perfectly determines depth label.\nThe observed 84.75\\% with monotonically decreasing per-depth F1 (d0=0.98, d1=0.88, d2=0.76, d3=0.76) is more consistent with a depth-signal interpretation, as architecture fingerprinting would not produce this graded pattern.\nWe address this confound directly with a reverse-chain control below.\n\n\\paragraph{Reverse-chain control.}\nTo empirically test the architecture confound hypothesis, we constructed a reverse chain (GPT-2~XL $\\to$ OLMo $\\to$ Pythia) reversing the model-depth assignment.\nThe reverse chain achieves 85.75\\% (95\\% CI [82.0, 89.25]), statistically indistinguishable from the forward chain (84.75\\%, [81.0, 88.25]).\nIf the classifier exploited architecture fingerprints rather than depth signal, reversing the chain should invert depth predictions; the equivalent accuracy under both orderings provides empirical evidence against the architecture confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-domain transfer.}\nAll prior experiments use WikiText-103 as the seed corpus.\nTo test domain dependence, we repeat the pipeline with arXiv abstracts as the seed text (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, 500 samples per depth, d0--d3).\nFive-fold cross-validation yields 74.1\\% $\\pm$ 2.0\\% accuracy, compared to ${\\sim}$78\\% on the WikiText seed.\nPer-depth F1 shows the familiar pattern: d0 = 1.00, d1 = 0.78, d2 = 0.52, d3 = 0.66, with d2 remaining the most challenging class.\nThe 4pp accuracy gap relative to WikiText likely reflects the narrower stylistic range of scientific abstracts, which may reduce the distributional contrast between adjacent depths.\nThese results provide preliminary evidence that the perplexity-based features capture properties of the recursive generation process rather than domain-specific artifacts of the seed corpus.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Replication}\n\\label{app:seed_replication}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed and cross-seed classification accuracy for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-seed transfer (Pythia-1.4B nucleus). Baseline seed=42 is the primary benchmark LoRA; seed=43 is an independent replication. $\\Delta$: difference from respective comparison point.}\n\\label{tab:seed_replication}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nWithin-seed (seed=42) & 78.20 & --- \\\\\nWithin-seed (seed=43) & 77.15 & $-$1.05$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCross-seed (train 42$\\to$test 43) & 77.85 & \\\\\nCross-seed (train 43$\\to$test 42) & 75.68 & \\\\\nCross-seed avg & 76.76 & $-$1.30$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$vs.\\ baseline (78.20\\%). $^\\ddagger$vs.\\ within-seed avg (78.07\\%).}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe seed=43 LoRA achieves 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15]), within 1.05\\,pp of the seed=42 baseline.\nCross-seed transfer averages 76.76\\%, a 1.30\\,pp deficit relative to within-seed performance.\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}, \\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}); depth signal therefore arises from the recursive process itself rather than idiosyncratic LoRA weight configurations.\n\n\\subsection{Generation Length Ablation}\n\\label{app:length_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Generation length ablation (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, 5-fold CV). Accuracy increases monotonically with passage length; the 9.47\\,pp spread confirms that longer passages provide stronger statistical signal.}\n\\label{tab:length_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPassage length (tokens) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n128 & 68.35 \\\\\n256 & 73.80 \\\\\n512 & 77.82 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDepth signal is detectable from 128 tokens (2.7$\\times$ chance) and saturates near 500 tokens.\nThe monotonic increase reflects the statistical nature of the features: longer passages yield more stable estimates of perplexity percentiles and surprisal variance, reducing within-class overlap at adjacent depth boundaries.\n\n\\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}\n\\label{app:mixed_depth}\n\nPer-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\\,pp tolerance).\nIndividual class deltas range from $-$0.45\\,pp to $-$2.43\\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift); the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.\nMacro-recall $\\Delta$ = $-$1.62\\,pp (within the 2\\,pp threshold); individual class variations (max 2.43\\,pp on d1) arise from held-out sample composition rather than classifier sensitivity to corpus distribution, as the Random Forest produces identical per-sample predictions regardless of batch context.\n\n\\subsection{Learned Estimator Comparison}\n\\label{app:learned_estimator}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:learned_estimator} evaluates three learned classifiers (XGBoost, ordinal MLP with CORAL loss, prototypical networks) under the same leave-one-model-out protocol as Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}.\nAll three methods operate on the same 19-dimensional feature vector; the comparison isolates the effect of the classification head.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Learned estimator cross-model transfer (leave-one-model-out, nucleus, 4-class). RF baseline from Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:learned_estimator}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL & Mean \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (baseline) & --- & --- & --- & 66.4 \\\\\nXGBoost & 63.7 & 69.8 & 68.1 & 67.2 \\\\\nOrdinal MLP & 62.5 & 69.7 & 67.9 & 66.7 \\\\\nPrototypical & 61.6 & 70.0 & 68.4 & 66.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll three learned methods match the RF baseline within 0.8\\,pp on average; the Pythia holdout is consistently the hardest (61.6--63.7\\%), while OLMo and GPT-2~XL holdouts yield 67.9--70.0\\%.\nThe narrow spread ($\\leq$0.8\\,pp across four methods) reinforces the conclusion that the 19-feature representation, not the classifier, determines the accuracy ceiling in the cross-model transfer setting.\n\n\\subsection{Instruction-Tuned Model at 1.5B Scale}\n\\label{app:instruct_1p5b}\n\nTo test whether instruction tuning eliminates depth signal at matched scale (1.5B, avoiding the quantization confound of \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), we run the full recursive pipeline on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with self-reference feature extraction (i.e., the same model serves as both generator and reference).\n\nThe RF classifier achieves 47.4\\% four-class accuracy (95\\% CI $[46.7, 48.1]$, $1.9\\times$ chance); instruction tuning therefore reduces but does not eliminate depth signal.\nThe pairwise AUC profile differs from base models: d0v1 = 0.653, d1v2 = 0.620, d2v3 = 0.664, with a gap-dependent pattern (non-adjacent pairs yield higher AUC, e.g., d0v3 = 0.902) but no pair reaching the 0.75 threshold.\n\nThe top-5 features shift substantially: \\texttt{surprisal\\_range}, \\texttt{surprisal\\_position\\_corr}, \\texttt{prob\\_std}, \\texttt{surprisal\\_first\\_half\\_minus\\_second}, and \\texttt{surprisal\\_std} replace the perplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) that dominate in base models.\nThis feature shift suggests instruction tuning compresses the perplexity distribution (reducing tail discriminability) while preserving positional and range-based surprisal patterns.\n\nTwo caveats apply: (1) self-reference evaluation may inflate or deflate accuracy relative to cross-reference evaluation (cf.\\ \\S\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); (2) a single instruction-tuned model does not establish the generality of the feature shift across architectures or instruction-tuning recipes.\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Fine-Tuning Probe}\n\\label{app:downstream}\n\nWe fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) on depth-stratified subsets (500 samples each) and evaluate held-out perplexity (200 samples, 50 per depth).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Downstream fine-tuning probe: held-out perplexity by training subset (Pythia-410M LoRA, seed=42). $\\Delta$: signed difference vs.\\ baseline (negative = better).}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nTraining subset & PPL & $\\Delta$ vs.\\ baseline \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (no fine-tuning) & 5.67 & --- \\\\\nd0 only & 5.65 & $-$0.02 \\\\\nd1 only & 5.55 & $-$0.12 \\\\\nd2 only & 5.56 & $-$0.11 \\\\\nMixed (d0--d3) & 5.55 & $-$0.12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe hypothesis that d0-only data yields the lowest perplexity is not supported: d1-only and mixed subsets tie for best (PPL~5.55), both marginally below d0-only (5.65).\nThe PPL spread is small (0.12, 2.1\\% relative) and the sample size (500 per condition, single seed) is insufficient for statistical significance.\nTraining loss decreases monotonically with depth (d0: 2.558, d1: 1.920, d2: 1.286), consistent with recursive generation reducing information content and making text easier to model.\nThis pilot establishes that depth-filtered data is not harmful for downstream fine-tuning; whether depth-aware curation provides a meaningful advantage requires larger-scale evaluation.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Group Ablation}\n\\label{app:feature_ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_ablation} reports cross-model transfer accuracy (leave-one-model-out, nucleus) when restricting the classifier to subsets of the 19-feature vector, grouped by signal type.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Feature group ablation (cross-model LOO, nucleus, 4-class). Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:feature_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nFeature group & \\#Feat. & Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ vs.\\ Full \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (all 19) & 19 & 66.6 & --- \\\\\nPerplexity only & 9 & 66.0 & $-$0.5 \\\\\nEntropy/Surprisal only & 5 & 63.2 & $-$3.4 \\\\\nRepetition/Diversity only & 5 & 53.0 & $-$13.6 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} only & 1 & 58.3 & $-$8.3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 9 perplexity features nearly match the full set ($\\Delta{=}{-}0.5$\\,pp), while repetition/diversity features alone fall 13.6\\,pp below; perplexity degradation statistics therefore carry the dominant depth signal.\nThe top-2 individual features by Gini importance are \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (22.4\\%) and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (18.8\\%), accounting for 41\\% of total importance.\n\n\\subsection{RF Seed Robustness}\n\\label{app:rf_seed}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:rf_seed} reports cross-model transfer accuracy across three RF random seeds.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{RF seed robustness (cross-model LOO, nucleus, 4-class).}\n\\label{tab:rf_seed}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nSeed & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n42 & 66.4 \\\\\n123 & 66.5 \\\\\n456 & 66.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean $\\pm$ std & 66.48 $\\pm$ 0.06 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 0.06\\,pp standard deviation across seeds confirms that RF classification is deterministic to within rounding noise at this sample size, and reported accuracy differences across experimental conditions (e.g., feature ablation, cross-model transfer) are not attributable to classifier randomness.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Detector Baselines for Ordinal Classification}\n\\label{app:detector_baselines}\n\nWe evaluate whether existing binary AI-text detectors, when their scores are used as features for ordinal classification, can match our 19-feature RF pipeline.\nBinoculars~\\cite{binoculars2024} and Fast-DetectGPT~\\cite{fast_detectgpt2024} scores are computed on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) subset and fed to: (a)~a log-likelihood rank classifier (Log-Rank, 64.0\\%), (b)~a combined RF trained on both detector scores (Combined, 74.2\\%), and (c)~the standard 19-feature RF (78.2\\%).\nThe 4\\,pp gap (74.2\\% vs.\\ 78.2\\%) confirms that perplexity distributional features capture ordinal depth structure beyond what generic detection scores provide.\nNote that Fast-DetectGPT exhibits numerical overflow on Pythia nucleus d1 (mean perturbation score $>$28{,}000); results for this cell should be interpreted cautiously.\n\n\\subsection{Prompted Generation Results}\n\\label{app:prompted_gen}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:prompted_gen} reports depth estimation performance under topic-seeded (prompted) generation using GPT-2~XL with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, depths 0--3).\nGeneration is conditioned on Wikipedia topic prompts rather than a bare BOS token, a more ecologically valid test of depth signal persistence.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Prompted generation (GPT-2~XL, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, topic-seeded). 5-fold CV accuracy and pairwise AUC. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:prompted_gen}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMetric & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-class accuracy (\\%) & 69.52 $[68.9, 70.2]$ \\\\\nBOS-seeded baseline (\\%) & 83.3 \\\\\n$\\Delta$ (prompted $-$ BOS) & $-$13.78\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nAUC d0v1 & 0.916 \\\\\nAUC d1v2 & 0.782 \\\\\nAUC d2v3 & 0.744 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTop-1 feature & \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} \\\\\nTop-2 feature & \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} \\\\\nTop-3 feature & \\texttt{p75\\_ppl} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 13.8\\,pp drop relative to BOS-seeded generation is expected: topic prompts constrain the output distribution, reducing the variance that distinguishes depths.\nNevertheless, accuracy remains $2.8\\times$ chance and pairwise AUC decays monotonically (0.916, 0.782, 0.744); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of unconstrained generation.\nTop features are identical to BOS-seeded (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl} first); the same perplexity tail erosion mechanism operates under prompted conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Extended Discussion}\n\\label{app:extended_discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}\nCurrent data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \\cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.\nRecursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.\nThe 70--85\\% in-distribution accuracy and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.\nImportance weighting \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.\n\n\\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}\nDepth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.\nSource attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.\nDepth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.\nA layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 667,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 667
    }
  }
}
```

```text
docs/paper/method.tex:47:The features fall into four categories (Table~\ref{tab:features}).
docs/paper/method.tex:72:\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\S\ref{sec:atlas}).
docs/paper/method.tex:85:For each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\lfloor\sqrt{19}\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\S\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.
docs/paper/method.tex:86:Each pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
docs/paper/method.tex:99:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:100:We set $T{=}0.75$ as default; sensitivity at $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ is reported in \S\ref{sec:atlas}.
docs/paper/method.tex:104:We compare against CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \S\ref{sec:generalization}.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:9:Table~\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates bagging (RF) and gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM) on the same 19 features; per-cell spread ($\leq$0.4pp) establishes a feature-determined ceiling.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:11:Binary detectors repurposed for ordinal classification (Binoculars + Fast-DetectGPT combined: 74.2\%) fall 4\,pp below RF (Appendix~\ref{app:detector_baselines}).
docs/paper/generalization.tex:14:Single-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\%); zero-shot LLMs (DeepSeek V3.2, 36.2\%; Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, 30.4\%) also fail (43\,pp below RF; Appendix~\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}); depth signal therefore resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:19:\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\Delta_{\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\%. RF values differ from Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\leq$0.2pp due to matched-fold evaluation. 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation $\leq$ 0.9\,pp across all settings.}
docs/paper/generalization.tex:32:Convergence confirms the signal resides in the features; three alternative classifiers match RF within 0.8\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:learned_estimator}).
docs/paper/generalization.tex:33:Perplexity features alone recover 66.0\% ($-$0.5\,pp vs.\ full), while repetition/diversity reach only 53.0\% ($-$13.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:feature_ablation}); perplexity degradation is the dominant depth marker.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:35:Figure~\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread to d1--d3 under transfer (seed std $= 0.06$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:rf_seed}).
docs/paper/generalization.tex:47:We perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
docs/paper/generalization.tex:73:Table~\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports leave-one-strategy-out transfer.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:94:A multi-temperature ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:multi_tau}) confirms within-$\tau$ signal for $\tau \leq 0.9$ but collapse at $\tau{=}1.1$; end-to-end PLMs lag RF by 20pp (Appendix~\ref{app:e2e}).
docs/paper/generalization.tex:97:Replicating the GPT-2~XL nucleus pipeline with topic-seeded generation yields 69.5\% (95\% CI $[68.9, 70.2]$, $-$13.8\,pp vs.\ BOS-seeded, $2.8\times$ chance), with monotonic AUC decay and identical top features (Appendix~\ref{app:prompted_gen}): prompt conditioning attenuates but does not eliminate the depth signal.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:104:A base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B, 59.9\%, non-monotonic AUC) vs.\ instruction-tuned (70.2\%, monotonic) suggests instruction tuning \emph{regularizes} recursive degradation; a 1.5B instruct control (47.4\%, $1.9\times$ chance) confirms at matched scale (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct_1p5b}).
docs/paper/generalization.tex:105:Cross-model chains (84.75\%), cross-domain (arXiv 74.1\%), and LoRA rank sensitivity ($\Delta{=}4.65$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) further confirm robustness.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:7:Figure~\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} in the main text shows feature trajectories for nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) and temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$) sampling.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:8:Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:15:To verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:24:Table~\ref{tab:per_class_recall} reports per-class recall across all 9 atlas cells (4-class in-distribution, 5-fold CV).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:53:Table~\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:93:The cross-strategy results in \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy} use a single temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:97:For $\tau \in [0.5, 0.9]$, d1-vs-d2 AUC exceeds 0.80 (peak 0.851 at $\tau{=}0.7$, comparable to nucleus $p{=}0.95$'s 0.861) and 4-class accuracy ranges from 75.4\% to 77.3\% (Table~\ref{tab:multi_tau_within}); the depth signal is therefore not an artifact of a single temperature.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:107:Leave-one-$\tau$-out transfer yields mean accuracy of 46.7\% with mean d1v2 AUC of 0.319 (Table~\ref{tab:multi_tau_cross}), worse than cross-strategy transfer (59.1\%; \S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:113:On noisier web-crawl data (5{,}000 C4 passages), FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL), concentrated in template/SEO content with high n-gram repetition (Table~\ref{tab:calibration}); deployment on heterogeneous web data requires model-specific calibration.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:156:This confirms that the 20pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\S\ref{app:e2e}) stems from complementary, not redundant, representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the targeted statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:162:The 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture bias; Table~\ref{tab:e2e_detail} shows both backbones share the same failure mode (high d0 F1, $>$0.94, but collapsed d1/d2 performance, F1 $\leq$ 0.42), while RF achieves 0.596 d2 recall, a $>$2$\times$ advantage at the hardest depth class.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:184:Three independent importance measures (SHAP, Gini, and permutation) show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \S\ref{app:permutation}), with \texttt{p90\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 8/9.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:185:Linear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \texttt{var\_surprisal} and \texttt{p90\_ppl} (\S\ref{app:feature_overlap}); pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics, a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:187:DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (\S\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods; depth estimation therefore benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:218:Table~\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages, crawled 2019--2020; pre-LLM deployment).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:253:Table~\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:316:This section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:321:Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:347:At threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$, exceeding the $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:367:Among instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:368:A base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\% four-class accuracy (95\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\% with strictly monotonic decay.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:434:Table~\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed and cross-seed classification accuracy for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:463:Table~\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:494:Table~\ref{tab:learned_estimator} evaluates three learned classifiers (XGBoost, ordinal MLP with CORAL loss, prototypical networks) under the same leave-one-model-out protocol as Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:500:\caption{Learned estimator cross-model transfer (leave-one-model-out, nucleus, 4-class). RF baseline from Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}. Chance = 25\%.}
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:520:To test whether instruction tuning eliminates depth signal at matched scale (1.5B, avoiding the quantization confound of \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), we run the full recursive pipeline on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with self-reference feature extraction (i.e., the same model serves as both generator and reference).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:528:Two caveats apply: (1) self-reference evaluation may inflate or deflate accuracy relative to cross-reference evaluation (cf.\ \S\ref{app:ref_ablation}); (2) a single instruction-tuned model does not establish the generality of the feature shift across architectures or instruction-tuning recipes.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:561:Table~\ref{tab:feature_ablation} reports cross-model transfer accuracy (leave-one-model-out, nucleus) when restricting the classifier to subsets of the 19-feature vector, grouped by signal type.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:588:Table~\ref{tab:rf_seed} reports cross-model transfer accuracy across three RF random seeds.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:621:Table~\ref{tab:prompted_gen} reports depth estimation performance under topic-seeded (prompted) generation using GPT-2~XL with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, depths 0--3).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:658:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/introduction.tex:18:We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce a lightweight pipeline: compute 19 reference-model perplexity features (effective dimensionality ${\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.
```

</details>

Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. class.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:184:Three independent importance measures (SHAP, Gini, and permutation) show strong concordance (Kendall's $W{=}0.90$, $p{<}0.005$; pairwise Spearman $\\rho$: SHAP--Gini $0.93$, SHAP--permutation $0.84$; \\S\\ref{app:permutation}), with \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} appearing in the SHAP top-2 in 9/9 cells and \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:185:Linear probing of PLM [CLS] representations yields negative $R^2$ for both \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} and \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (\\S\\ref{app:feature_overlap}); pretrained representations lack the linear structure needed to recover perplexity tail statistics, a systematic representational gap rather than a backbone-specific artifact.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:187:DCOL \\cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\\% (\\S\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\\%) underperform feature-based methods; depth estimation therefore benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:218:Table~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages, crawled 2019--2020; pre-LLM deployment).\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:253:Table~\\ref{tab:curation_poc} reports per-condition perplexity; Table~\\ref{tab:curation_comparisons} reports pairwise contrasts.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:316:This section provides the full cross-scale analysis summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:321:Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale} contrasts the two 7--8B models with the three 1--1.5B models on matched evaluation (nucleus sampling, depths 0--3).\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:347:At threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$, exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:367:Among instruction-tuned models, Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic AUC decay consistent with the 1--1.5B pattern, while Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped recovery beyond d2v3 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}).\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:368:A base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B without instruction tuning) clarifies the causal direction: the base model achieves 59.9\\% four-class accuracy (95\\% CI [57.8, 62.15], $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$) with non-monotonic AUC decay (Table~\\ref{tab:llama3_base_auc}), while the instruction-tuned variant reaches 70.2\\% with strictly monotonic decay.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:434:Table~\\ref{tab:seed_replication} reports within-seed and cross-seed classification accuracy for Pythia-1.4B (nucleus, depths 0--3) under two independent LoRA training seeds.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:463:Table~\\ref{tab:length_ablation} reports classification accuracy as a function of passage length (Pythia-1.4B nucleus, depths 0--3).\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:494:Table~\\ref{tab:learned_estimator} evaluates three learned classifiers (XGBoost, ordinal MLP with CORAL loss, prototypical networks) under the same leave-one-model-out protocol as Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:500:\\caption{Learned estimator cross-model transfer (leave-one-model-out, nucleus, 4-class). RF baseline from Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}. Chance = 25\\%.}\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:520:To test whether instruction tuning eliminates depth signal at matched scale (1.5B, avoiding the quantization confound of \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}), we run the full recursive pipeline on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with self-reference feature extraction (i.e., the same model serves as both generator and reference).\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:528:Two caveats apply: (1) self-reference evaluation may inflate or deflate accuracy relative to cross-reference evaluation (cf.\\ \\S\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); (2) a single instruction-tuned model does not establish the generality of the feature shift across architectures or instruction-tuning recipes.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:561:Table~\\ref{tab:feature_ablation} reports cross-model transfer accuracy (leave-one-model-out, nucleus) when restricting the classifier to subsets of the 19-feature vector, grouped by signal type.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:588:Table~\\ref{tab:rf_seed} reports cross-model transfer accuracy across three RF random seeds.\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:621:Table~\\ref{tab:prompted_gen} reports depth estimation performance under topic-seeded (prompted) generation using GPT-2~XL with nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$, depths 0--3).\ndocs/paper/section_appendix.tex:658:[Omitted long matching line]\ndocs/paper/introduction.tex:18:We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce a lightweight pipeline: compute 19 reference-model perplexity features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.",

```text
docs/paper/related_work.tex:5:\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails; \citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this via scaling laws \cite{seddik2024synthetic}, \citet{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and the MAD framework \cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends the analysis to self-consuming models.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:6:Subsequent work addresses mitigation \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate,drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse,yi2026_escaping_collapse,falahati2026_curated,wenzek2020_ccnet}, non-monotonic dynamics \cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, convergence \cite{marchi2024_heat_death,wang2026_contamination,knowledge_collapse2025,dohmatob2024_demystified}, and diversity decline \cite{guo2024curious,briesch2024selfconsuming,padmakumar2024diversity,sigma2026_spectral,synthetic_eggs2026}.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:7:\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple distinct phenomena, and call for operationally precise metrics.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:8:Dohmatob et al.\ predict that recursive training erodes distributional tails as a theoretical scaling law \citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; we operationalize this prediction into a deployable ordinal classifier, revealing strategy-dependent trajectories and non-monotonic 7B behavior that the theory does not predict.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:12:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/related_work.tex:13:M4-SemEval \cite{wang2024_m4_semeval}, RAID \cite{dugan2024_raid}, and MAGE \cite{li2024_mage} benchmark detection across domains, models, and adversarial conditions.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:17:EditLens \cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains; \citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \emph{which} model generated text; LOKI \cite{loki2025} benchmarks synthetic data detection across modalities.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:18:\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} study iterative generation as a ``broken telephone'' effect, characterizing \emph{how} text degrades without classifying which iteration a passage comes from.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:22:Ordinal classification has a long history \cite{mccullagh1980_ordinal}: \citet{frank2001} reduce ordinal problems to nested binary tasks, and \citet{rennie2005} introduce loss functions tailored to discrete ordered labels.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:23:CORAL \cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:27:Decoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}.
docs/paper/atlas.tex:62:This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
docs/paper/atlas.tex:170:Temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$) yields the highest accuracy in 2/3 models (GPT-2~XL 85.9\%, OLMo 82.1\%) because uniform logit scaling accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint. However, this acceleration creates a boundary reversal risk: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features, causing cross-strategy classifiers to systematically overpredict depth (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
docs/paper/acl.sty:190:\renewcommand\cite{\citep}  % to get "(Author Year)" with natbib
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:30:Adversarial paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:34:However, \citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:55:Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:5:LoRA fine-tuning loss decreases monotonically with depth for all three families, a direct empirical validation of the tail erosion predicted by \citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; instruction tuning regularizes this compression (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}).
docs/paper/acl_natbib.bst:55: %   \citet{key} ==>>                Jones et al. (1990)
docs/paper/acl_natbib.bst:57: %   \citep{key} ==>>                (Jones et al., 1990)
docs/paper/acl_natbib.bst:1053:  " \cite{" * crossref * "}" *
docs/paper/acl_natbib.bst:1065:  " \cite{" * crossref * "}" *

docs/paper/method.tex:24:Each model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).
docs/paper/method.tex:33:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/method.tex:104:We compare against CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \S\ref{sec:generalization}.
docs/paper/generalization.tex:10:On Pythia nucleus, CORAL \cite{coral2020} (78.1\%) and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} (78.2\%) match RF within 0.6\,pp ($p>0.05$); all feature-based methods outperform CE-MLP (74.9\%) and logistic regression (75.0\%).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:161:DeBERTa-v3-base \cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \cite{liu2019_roberta} finetuned end-to-end achieve 58.3\% ($\pm$4.7\%) and 59.5\% ($\pm$3.2\%) respectively, lagging the RF baseline (78.7\%) by 20.4pp and 19.2pp.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:187:DCOL \cite{cloc2025} reached 64.5\% (\S\ref{app:dcol_iterations}); all end-to-end approaches (58--65\%) underperform feature-based methods; depth estimation therefore benefits from targeted statistical features that capture distributional tail properties absent from PLM representations.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:614:Binoculars~\cite{binoculars2024} and Fast-DetectGPT~\cite{fast_detectgpt2024} scores are computed on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) subset and fed to: (a)~a log-likelihood rank classifier (Log-Rank, 64.0\%), (b)~a combined RF trained on both detector scores (Combined, 74.2\%), and (c)~the standard 19-feature RF (78.2\%).
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:656:Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:659:Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:663:Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:664:Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
docs/paper/section_appendix.tex:680:LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, lr $2{\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs) follow \citet{hu2022lora}.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:5:As synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:6:This model$\to$data$\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:9:Binary detectors such as Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \geq 1$ into a single category.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:10:Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:11:The model collapse literature \cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.
```
